<p><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="Seal of the President of the United States" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/70px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png" decoding="async" width="70" height="70" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/105px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg/140px-Seal_of_the_President_of_the_United_States.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="2424" data-file-height="2425" /></span></span>

<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/List_of_personal_coats_of_arms_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="List of personal coats of arms of presidents of the United States"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="52" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Chester_A._Arthur.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="395" data-file-height="516" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Chester_A._Arthur_series" title="Template:Chester A. Arthur series"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Chester_A._Arthur_series" title="Template talk:Chester A. Arthur series"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Chester_A._Arthur_series&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Chester Alan Arthur</b> (October 5, 1829<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> – November 18, 1886) was an American politician who served as the 21st <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1881 to 1885. He was a <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican</a> lawyer from <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a> who previously served as the 20th <a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">vice president</a> under President <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a>. Arthur assumed the presidency after Garfield's <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_James_A._Garfield" title="Assassination of James A. Garfield">death</a> on September 19, 1881, and served the remainder of his term until March 4, 1885.
</p><p>Arthur was born in <a href="/wiki/Fairfield,_Vermont" title="Fairfield, Vermont">Fairfield, Vermont</a>, grew up in <a href="/wiki/Upstate_New_York" title="Upstate New York">upstate New York</a> and practiced law in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>. He served as quartermaster general of the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Army_National_Guard" title="New York Army National Guard">New York Militia</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>. Following the war, he devoted more time to New York Republican politics and quickly rose in Senator <a href="/wiki/Roscoe_Conkling" title="Roscoe Conkling">Roscoe Conkling</a>'s political organization. President <a href="/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant" title="Ulysses S. Grant">Ulysses S. Grant</a> appointed him to the post of <a href="/wiki/Collector_of_the_Port_of_New_York" title="Collector of the Port of New York">Collector of the Port of New York</a> in 1871, and he was an important supporter of Conkling and the <a href="/wiki/Stalwart_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Stalwart (politics)">Stalwart</a> faction of the Republican Party. In 1878, following bitter disputes with President <a href="/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes" title="Rutherford B. Hayes">Rutherford B. Hayes</a>, Hayes fired Arthur as part of a plan to reform the federal patronage system in New York. In <a href="/wiki/1880_Republican_National_Convention" title="1880 Republican National Convention">June 1880</a>, the Republican Party nominated Arthur for vice president to balance the ticket as an Eastern Stalwart. Arthur and presidential nominee James A. Garfield won the <a href="/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election" title="1880 United States presidential election">1880 presidential election</a> and took their respective offices in March 1881. Four months into his term, President Garfield was shot by an assassin; he died 11 weeks later, and Arthur assumed the presidency.
</p><p>As president, Arthur presided over the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_Navy#&quot;New_Navy&quot;" title="History of the United States Navy">rebirth</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Navy" title="United States Navy">US Navy</a>, but he was criticized for failing to alleviate the federal budget surplus which had been accumulating since the end of the Civil War. Arthur vetoed the first version of the 1882 <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act" title="Chinese Exclusion Act">Chinese Exclusion Act</a>, arguing that its twenty-year ban on Chinese immigrants to the United States violated the <a href="/wiki/Burlingame_Treaty" title="Burlingame Treaty">Burlingame Treaty</a>, but he signed a second version, which included a ten-year ban.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He appointed <a href="/wiki/Horace_Gray" title="Horace Gray">Horace Gray</a> and <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Blatchford" title="Samuel Blatchford">Samuel Blatchford</a> to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Supreme Court">Supreme Court</a>. He also enforced the <a href="/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1882" title="Immigration Act of 1882">Immigration Act of 1882</a> to impose more restrictions on immigrants and the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1883" title="Tariff of 1883">Tariff of 1883</a> to attempt to reduce <a href="/wiki/Tariffs" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs">tariffs</a>. Arthur signed into law the <a href="/wiki/Pendleton_Civil_Service_Reform_Act" title="Pendleton Civil Service Reform Act">Pendleton Civil Service Reform Act</a> of 1883, which came as a surprise to reformers who held a negative reputation of Arthur as a Stalwart and product of Conkling's organization.
</p><p>Suffering from poor health, Arthur made only a limited effort to secure the Republican Party's nomination in 1884, and he retired at the end of his term. Arthur's failing health and political temperament combined to make his administration less active than a modern presidency, yet he earned praise among contemporaries for his solid performance in office. Journalist <a href="/wiki/Alexander_McClure" title="Alexander McClure">Alexander McClure</a> wrote, "No man ever entered the Presidency so profoundly and widely distrusted as Chester Alan Arthur, and no one ever retired&#160;... more generally respected, alike by political friend and foe."<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> 
The <i><a href="/wiki/New_York_World" title="New York World">New York World</a></i> summed up Arthur's presidency at his death in 1886: "No duty was neglected in his administration, and no adventurous project alarmed the nation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975423_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975423-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Mark_Twain" title="Mark Twain">Mark Twain</a> wrote of him, "It would be hard indeed to better President Arthur's administration."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman95_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman95-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Evaluations by modern historians generally rank Arthur as a mediocre or <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#Notable_scholar_surveys" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">average</a> president.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Arthur has also been described as one of the <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#Memorability_of_the_presidents" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">least memorable</a> presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family">Birth and family</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG/200px-Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG/300px-Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG/400px-Fairfield_ChesterArthur_Nov2007.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Arthur's birthplace in <a href="/wiki/Fairfield,_Vermont" title="Fairfield, Vermont">Fairfield, Vermont</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Chester Alan Arthur was born in <a href="/wiki/Fairfield,_Vermont" title="Fairfield, Vermont">Fairfield, Vermont</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Arthur's mother, Malvina Stone was born in <a href="/wiki/Berkshire,_Vermont" title="Berkshire, Vermont">Berkshire, Vermont</a>, the daughter of George Washington Stone and Judith Stevens.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHambley103_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHambley103-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Her family was primarily of <a href="/wiki/English_Americans" title="English Americans">English</a> and <a href="/wiki/Welsh_Americans" title="Welsh Americans">Welsh</a> descent, and her maternal grandfather, Uriah Stone, had served in the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Army" title="Continental Army">Continental Army</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's father, <a href="/wiki/William_Arthur_(clergyman)" title="William Arthur (clergyman)">William Arthur</a>, was born in 1796 in Dreen, <a href="/wiki/Cullybackey" title="Cullybackey">Cullybackey</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">County Antrim</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a> to a <a href="/wiki/Presbyterianism" title="Presbyterianism">Presbyterian</a> family of <a href="/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans" title="Scotch-Irish Americans">Scots-Irish</a> descent. William's mother was born Eliza McHarg and she married Alan Arthur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> William graduated from college in <a href="/wiki/Belfast" title="Belfast">Belfast</a> and migrated to the <a href="/wiki/Lower_Canada" title="Lower Canada">Province of Lower Canada</a> in 1819 or 1820.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReevesJuly_1,_1970179_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReevesJuly_1,_1970179-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Malvina Stone met William Arthur when Arthur was teaching school in <a href="/wiki/Dunham,_Quebec" title="Dunham, Quebec">Dunham, Quebec</a>, near the Vermont border.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)294_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)294-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> They married in Dunham on April 12, 1821, soon after meeting.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Arthurs moved to Vermont after the birth of their first child, Regina.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)294_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)294-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> They quickly moved from <a href="/wiki/Burlington,_Vermont" title="Burlington, Vermont">Burlington</a> to <a href="/wiki/Jericho,_Vermont" title="Jericho, Vermont">Jericho</a>, and finally to <a href="/wiki/Waterville,_Vermont" title="Waterville, Vermont">Waterville</a>, as William received positions teaching at different schools.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> William Arthur also spent a brief time studying law, but while still in Waterville, he departed from both his legal studies and his <a href="/wiki/Presbyterianism" title="Presbyterianism">Presbyterian</a> upbringing to join the <a href="/wiki/Free_Will_Baptist" title="Free Will Baptist">Free Will Baptists</a>; he spent the rest of his life as a minister in that sect.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19754Howe4-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> William Arthur became an outspoken <a href="/wiki/Abolitionist" class="mw-redirect" title="Abolitionist">abolitionist</a>, which often made him unpopular with some members of his congregations and contributed to the family's frequent moves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe7Reeves19756_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe7Reeves19756-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1828, the family moved again, to Fairfield, where Chester Alan Arthur was born the following year; he was the fifth of nine children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19755_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19755-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe5,_25,_28,_29_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe5,_25,_28,_29-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He was named "Chester" after Chester Abell,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVermont_Bureau_of_Publicity84_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermont_Bureau_of_Publicity84-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> the physician and family friend who assisted in his birth, and "Alan" for his paternal grandfather.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975436_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975436-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> The family remained in Fairfield until 1832, when William Arthur's profession took them to churches in several towns in Vermont and upstate New York. The family finally settled in the <a href="/wiki/Schenectady,_New_York" title="Schenectady, New York">Schenectady, New York</a>, area.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur had seven siblings who lived to adulthood:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHudson246_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudson246-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Regina (1822–1910), the wife of William G. Caw, a grocer, banker, and community leader of <a href="/wiki/Cohoes,_New_York" title="Cohoes, New York">Cohoes, New York</a>, who served as <a href="/wiki/Town_supervisor" class="mw-redirect" title="Town supervisor">town supervisor</a> and village trustee<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Sister_of_Arthur_Dead&quot;_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Sister_of_Arthur_Dead&quot;-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Jane (1824–1842)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeldman13_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman13-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Almeda (1825–1899), the wife of James H. Masten who served as postmaster of Cohoes and publisher of the <i>Cohoes Cataract</i> newspaper<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDolton_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDolton-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Ann (1828–1915), a career educator who taught school in New York and worked in <a href="/wiki/South_Carolina" title="South Carolina">South Carolina</a> in the years immediately before and after the Civil War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Burlington_Free_Press&quot;_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Burlington_Free_Press&quot;-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Malvina (1832–1920), the wife of Henry J. Haynesworth who was an official of the <a href="/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America" title="Confederate States of America">Confederate</a> government and a merchant in <a href="/wiki/Albany,_New_York" title="Albany, New York">Albany, New York</a>, before being appointed as a <a href="/wiki/Captain_(United_States_O-3)" title="Captain (United States O-3)">captain</a> and assistant <a href="/wiki/Quartermaster#United_States_Army" title="Quartermaster">quartermaster</a> in the U.S. Army during Arthur's presidency<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReevesJuly_1,_1970184_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReevesJuly_1,_1970184-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>William (1834–1915), a medical school graduate who became a career Army officer and paymaster, he was wounded during his Civil War service. William Arthur retired in 1898 with the brevet rank of <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_colonel_(United_States)" title="Lieutenant colonel (United States)">lieutenant colonel</a>, and permanent rank of <a href="/wiki/Major_(United_States)" title="Major (United States)">major</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJenks310_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenks310-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>George (1836–1838)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)295_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)295-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mary_Arthur_McElroy" title="Mary Arthur McElroy">Mary</a> (1841–1917), the wife of John E. McElroy, an Albany businessman and insurance executive, and Arthur's official White House hostess during his presidency<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Mrs._John_E._McElroy_Dead&quot;_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Mrs._John_E._McElroy_Dead&quot;-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>The family's frequent moves later spawned accusations that Arthur was not a native-born citizen of the United States. When Arthur was <a href="/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election" title="1880 United States presidential election">nominated for vice president in 1880</a>, a New York attorney and political opponent, Arthur P. Hinman, initially speculated that Arthur was born in Ireland and did not come to the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> until he was fourteen years old.<sup id="cite_ref-Bushnell_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bushnell-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Had that been true, opponents might have argued that Arthur was ineligible for the vice presidency under the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">United States Constitution</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Natural-born-citizen_clause_(United_States)" title="Natural-born-citizen clause (United States)">natural-born-citizen clause</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell53–54_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell53–54-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFisher28_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher28-36">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975202–203_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975202–203-38">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> When Hinman's original story did not take root, he spread a new rumor that Arthur was born in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-Bushnell_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bushnell-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> This claim, too, failed to gain credence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975202–203_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975202–203-38">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)292–293_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves_(Autumn_1970)292–293-40">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h3>
<p>Arthur spent some of his childhood years living in the New York towns of <a href="/wiki/York,_New_York" title="York, New York">York</a>, <a href="/wiki/Perry,_New_York" title="Perry, New York">Perry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greenwich_(town),_New_York" title="Greenwich (town), New York">Greenwich</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lansingburgh,_New_York" title="Lansingburgh, New York">Lansingburgh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Schenectady,_New_York" title="Schenectady, New York">Schenectady</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hoosick,_New_York" title="Hoosick, New York">Hoosick</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerris1999127_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerris1999127-41">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> One of his first teachers said Arthur was a boy "frank and open in manners and genial in disposition."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe7_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe7-42">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> During his time at school, he gained his first political inclinations and supported the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>. He joined other young Whigs in support of <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>, even participating in a brawl against students who supported <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> during the <a href="/wiki/1844_United_States_presidential_election" title="1844 United States presidential election">1844 United States presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Arthur also supported the <a href="/wiki/Fenian_Brotherhood" title="Fenian Brotherhood">Fenian Brotherhood</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Irish_republicanism" title="Irish republicanism">Irish republican</a> organization founded in America; he showed this support by wearing a green coat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19759_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19759-43">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> After completing his college preparation at the Lyceum of Union Village (now <a href="/wiki/Greenwich_(village),_New_York" title="Greenwich (village), New York">Greenwich</a>) and a grammar school in Schenectady, Arthur enrolled at <a href="/wiki/Union_College" title="Union College">Union College</a> there in 1845, where he studied the traditional <a href="/wiki/Classical_education_movement" title="Classical education movement">classical curriculum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19757–8-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Psi_Upsilon" title="Psi Upsilon">Psi Upsilon</a> <a href="/wiki/Fraternities_and_sororities" title="Fraternities and sororities">fraternity</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> and as a <a href="/wiki/Senior_(education)#Higher_education" title="Senior (education)">senior</a> he was president of the debate society and was elected to <a href="/wiki/Phi_Beta_Kappa" title="Phi Beta Kappa">Phi Beta Kappa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19759_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19759-43">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> During his winter breaks, he served as a teacher at a school in <a href="/wiki/Schaghticoke_(town),_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Schaghticoke (town), New York">Schaghticoke</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19759_43-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19759-43">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg/200px-Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg/300px-Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg/400px-Chester_A._Arthur_home.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1440" data-file-height="2160" /></a><figcaption>The home in <a href="/wiki/Manhattan" title="Manhattan">Manhattan</a> where Arthur spent most of his adulthood years</figcaption></figure>
<p>After graduating in 1848, Arthur returned to Schaghticoke and became a full-time teacher, and soon began to pursue an education in law.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197510_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197510-45">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> While studying law, he continued teaching, moving closer to home by taking a job at a school in <a href="/wiki/North_Pownal,_Vermont" title="North Pownal, Vermont">North Pownal, Vermont</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197510_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197510-45">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Coincidentally, future president <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a> taught penmanship at the same school three years later, but the two did not cross paths during their teaching careers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197511_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197511-46">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1852, Arthur moved again, to <a href="/wiki/Cohoes,_New_York" title="Cohoes, New York">Cohoes, New York</a>, to become the principal of a school at which his sister, Malvina, was a teacher.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197511_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197511-46">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1853, after studying at <a href="/wiki/State_and_National_Law_School" title="State and National Law School">State and National Law School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ballston_Spa,_New_York" title="Ballston Spa, New York">Ballston Spa, New York</a>, and then saving enough money to relocate, Arthur moved to New York City to <a href="/wiki/Reading_law" title="Reading law">read law</a> at the office of <a href="/wiki/Erastus_D._Culver" title="Erastus D. Culver">Erastus D. Culver</a>, an abolitionist lawyer and family friend.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell12_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell12-47">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> When Arthur was <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_bar_in_the_United_States" title="Admission to the bar in the United States">admitted to the New York bar</a> in 1854, he joined Culver's firm, which was subsequently renamed Culver, Parker, and Arthur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197514_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197514-48">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_York_lawyer">New York lawyer</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Black-and-white photograph of a young man with a mustache" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png/170px-Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png/255px-Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9d/Chester_A_Arthur_1859.png 2x" data-file-width="320" data-file-height="390" /></a><figcaption>Arthur as a young lawyer</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ellen_Arthur.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Black-and-white photograph of a woman with dark hair" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Ellen_Arthur.jpg/170px-Ellen_Arthur.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Ellen_Arthur.jpg/255px-Ellen_Arthur.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Ellen_Arthur.jpg/340px-Ellen_Arthur.jpg 2x" data-file-width="440" data-file-height="585" /></a><figcaption>Arthur married <a href="/wiki/Nell_Arthur" title="Nell Arthur">Ellen Herndon</a> in 1859.</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Arthur joined the firm, Culver and New York attorney <a href="/wiki/John_Jay_(lawyer)" title="John Jay (lawyer)">John Jay</a> (the grandson of the Founding Father <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a>) were pursuing a <i><a href="/wiki/Habeas_corpus" title="Habeas corpus">habeas corpus</a></i> action against Jonathan Lemmon, a Virginia slaveholder who was passing through New York with his eight slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15-49">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In <i><a href="/wiki/Lemmon_v._New_York" title="Lemmon v. New York">Lemmon v. New York</a></i>, Culver argued that, as New York law did not permit slavery, any slave arriving in New York was automatically freed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15-49">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The argument was successful, and after several appeals was upheld by the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Court_of_Appeals" title="New York Court of Appeals">New York Court of Appeals</a> in 1860.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197514–15-49">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Campaign biographers would later give Arthur much of the credit for the victory; in fact his role was minor, although he was certainly an active participant in the case.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197516_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197516-50">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In another civil rights case in 1854, Arthur was the lead attorney representing <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Jennings_Graham" title="Elizabeth Jennings Graham">Elizabeth Jennings Graham</a> after she was denied a seat on a streetcar because she was black.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197516_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197516-50">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He won the case, and the verdict led to the <a href="/wiki/Desegregation_in_the_United_States" title="Desegregation in the United States">desegregation</a> of the New York City streetcar lines.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197516_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197516-50">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1856, Arthur courted <a href="/wiki/Nell_Arthur" title="Nell Arthur">Ellen Herndon</a>, the daughter of <a href="/wiki/William_Lewis_Herndon" title="William Lewis Herndon">William Lewis Herndon</a>, a Virginia naval officer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197519–20_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197519–20-51">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The two were soon engaged to be married.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell14_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell14-52">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, he started a new law partnership with a friend, Henry D. Gardiner, and traveled with him to Kansas to consider purchasing land and setting up a law practice there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197516_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197516-50">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> At that time, the state was the scene of a <a href="/wiki/Bleeding_Kansas" title="Bleeding Kansas">brutal struggle</a> between pro-slavery and anti-slavery forces, and Arthur lined up firmly with the latter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197517–18_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197517–18-53">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The rough frontier life did not agree with the genteel New Yorkers; after three or four months the two young lawyers returned to New York City, where Arthur comforted his fiancée after her father was lost at sea in the wreck of the <a href="/wiki/SS_Central_America" title="SS Central America">SS <i>Central America</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197517–18_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197517–18-53">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In 1859, they were married at <a href="/wiki/Calvary_Church_(Manhattan)" title="Calvary Church (Manhattan)">Calvary Episcopal Church</a> in Manhattan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197521_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197521-54">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The couple had three children:
</p>
<ul><li>William Lewis Arthur (December 10, 1860&#160;– July 7, 1863), died of "<a href="/wiki/Convulsions" class="mw-redirect" title="Convulsions">convulsions</a>"</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chester_Alan_Arthur_II" title="Chester Alan Arthur II">Chester Alan Arthur II</a> (July 25, 1864&#160;– July 18, 1937), married Myra Townsend, then Rowena Graves, father of <a href="/wiki/Gavin_Arthur" title="Gavin Arthur">Gavin Arthur</a></li>
<li>Ellen Hansbrough Herndon "Nell" Arthur Pinkerton (November 21, 1871&#160;– September 6, 1915), married Charles Pinkerton</li></ul>
<p>After his marriage, Arthur devoted his efforts to building his law practice, but also found time to engage in Republican party politics. In addition, he indulged his military interest by becoming <a href="/wiki/Judge_Advocate_General%27s_Corps,_United_States_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Judge Advocate General&#39;s Corps, United States Army">Judge Advocate General</a> for the Second Brigade of the New York Militia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe18–19_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe18–19-55">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civil_War">Civil War</span></h3>
<p>In 1861, Arthur was appointed to the military staff of Governor <a href="/wiki/Edwin_D._Morgan" title="Edwin D. Morgan">Edwin D. Morgan</a> as engineer-in-chief.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe18–19_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe18–19-55">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The office was a patronage appointment of minor importance until the outbreak of the Civil War in April 1861, when New York and the other northern states were faced with raising and equipping armies of a size never before seen in American history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe20–21Reeves197522–23_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe20–21Reeves197522–23-56">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Arthur was commissioned as a <a href="/wiki/Brigadier_general_(United_States)" title="Brigadier general (United States)">brigadier general</a> and assigned to the state militia's <a href="/wiki/Quartermaster" title="Quartermaster">quartermaster</a> department.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe20–21Reeves197522–23_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe20–21Reeves197522–23-56">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> He was so efficient at housing and outfitting the troops that poured into New York City that he was promoted to inspector general of the state militia in March 1862, and then to quartermaster general that July.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197524–25_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197524–25-57">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> He had an opportunity to serve at the front when the <a href="/wiki/9th_New_York_Volunteer_Infantry_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="9th New York Volunteer Infantry Regiment">9th New York Volunteer Infantry Regiment</a> elected him commander with the rank of <a href="/wiki/Colonel" title="Colonel">colonel</a> early in the war, but at Governor Morgan's request, he turned it down to remain at his post in New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe25_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe25-58">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He also turned down command of four New York City regiments organized as the Metropolitan Brigade, again at Morgan's request.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe25_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe25-58">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The closest Arthur came to the front was when he traveled south to inspect New York troops near <a href="/wiki/Fredericksburg,_Virginia" title="Fredericksburg, Virginia">Fredericksburg</a>, Virginia, in May 1862, shortly after forces under Major General <a href="/wiki/Irvin_McDowell" title="Irvin McDowell">Irvin McDowell</a> seized the town during the <a href="/wiki/Peninsula_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Peninsula Campaign">Peninsula Campaign</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe26–27Reeves197528–29_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe26–27Reeves197528–29-59">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> That summer, he and other representatives of northern governors met with Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/William_H._Seward" title="William H. Seward">William H. Seward</a> in New York to coordinate the raising of additional troops, and he spent the next few months helping to enlist New York's quota of 120,000 men.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe26–27Reeves197528–29_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe26–27Reeves197528–29-59">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Arthur received plaudits for his work, but his post was a political appointment, and he was relieved of his militia duties in January 1863 when Governor <a href="/wiki/Horatio_Seymour" title="Horatio Seymour">Horatio Seymour</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democrat</a>, took office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197530_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197530-60">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> When <a href="/wiki/Reuben_Fenton" title="Reuben Fenton">Reuben Fenton</a> won the 1864 election for governor, Arthur requested reappointment; Fenton and Arthur were from different factions of the Republican Party, and Fenton had already committed to appointing another candidate, so Arthur did not return to military service.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197533_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197533-61">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur returned to practicing law, and with the help of additional contacts made in the military, he and the firm of Arthur &amp; Gardiner flourished.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe30–31Reeves197533–34_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe30–31Reeves197533–34-62">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Even as his professional life improved, however, Arthur and his wife experienced a personal tragedy as their only child, William, died suddenly that year at the age of two.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe29–30Reeves197534–35_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe29–30Reeves197534–35-63">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The couple took their son's death hard, and when they had another son, Chester Alan Jr., in 1864, they lavished attention on him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197535_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197535-64">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> They also had a daughter, Ellen, in 1871.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197584_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197584-65">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Both children survived to adulthood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197584_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197584-65">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's political prospects improved along with his law practice when his patron, ex-Governor Morgan, was elected to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197537_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197537-66">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> He was hired by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Murphy_(Collector)" title="Thomas Murphy (Collector)">Thomas Murphy</a>, a Republican politician, but also a friend of <a href="/wiki/William_M._Tweed" title="William M. Tweed">William M. Tweed</a>, the boss of the <a href="/wiki/Tammany_Hall" title="Tammany Hall">Tammany Hall</a> Democratic organization. Murphy was also a <a href="/wiki/Hatmaking" title="Hatmaking">hatter</a> who sold goods to the Union Army, and Arthur represented him in Washington. The two became associates within New York Republican party circles, eventually rising in the ranks of the conservative branch of the party dominated by <a href="/wiki/Thurlow_Weed" title="Thurlow Weed">Thurlow Weed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197537_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197537-66">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1864_United_States_presidential_election" title="1864 United States presidential election">presidential election of 1864</a>, Arthur and Murphy raised funds from Republicans in New York, and they attended the <a href="/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Abraham_Lincoln" title="Second inauguration of Abraham Lincoln">second inauguration of Abraham Lincoln</a> in 1865.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197538_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197538-67">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="New_York_politician">New York politician</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Conkling.27s_machine"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Conkling's_machine">Conkling's machine</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A columned building with a domed roof" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg/250px-NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="186" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg/375px-NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg/500px-NYCustomHouseMerchantsExchange.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="475" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/New_York_Custom_House" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Custom House">New York Custom House</a> (formerly the Merchants' Exchange building at <a href="/wiki/55_Wall_Street" title="55 Wall Street">55 Wall Street</a>) was Arthur's office for seven years.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The end of the Civil War meant new opportunities for the men in Morgan's Republican <a href="/wiki/Political_machine" title="Political machine">machine</a>, including Arthur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell17_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell17-68">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Morgan leaned toward the conservative wing of the New York Republican party, as did the men who worked with him in the organization, including Weed, Seward (who continued in office under President <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a>), and <a href="/wiki/Roscoe_Conkling" title="Roscoe Conkling">Roscoe Conkling</a> (an eloquent <a href="/wiki/Utica,_New_York" title="Utica, New York">Utica</a> Congressman and rising star in the party).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell17_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell17-68">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Arthur rarely articulated his own political ideas during his time as a part of the machine; as was common at the time, loyalty and hard work on the machine's behalf was more important than actual political positions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197539Howe37_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197539Howe37-69">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the time, U.S. <a href="/wiki/Custom_house" title="Custom house">custom houses</a> were managed by political appointees who served as Collector, Naval Officer, and Surveyor. In 1866, Arthur unsuccessfully attempted to secure the position of Naval Officer at the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Custom_House" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Custom House">New York Custom House</a>, a lucrative job subordinate only to the Collector.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197540–41_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197540–41-70">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> He continued his law practice (now a solo practice after Gardiner's death) and his role in politics, becoming a member of the prestigious <a href="/wiki/Century_Association" title="Century Association">Century Club</a> in 1867.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197540–41_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197540–41-70">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Conkling, elected to the United States Senate <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate_election_in_New_York,_1867" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Senate election in New York, 1867">in 1867</a>, noticed Arthur and facilitated his rise in the party, and Arthur became chairman of the New York City Republican executive committee in 1868.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197542–45_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197542–45-71">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> His ascent in the party hierarchy kept him busy most nights, and his wife resented his continual absence from the family home on party business.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197571–73_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197571–73-72">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Conkling succeeded to leadership of the conservative wing of New York's Republicans by 1868 as Morgan concentrated more time and effort on national politics, including serving as chairman of the <a href="/wiki/Republican_National_Committee" title="Republican National Committee">Republican National Committee</a>. The Conkling machine was solidly behind General <a href="/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant" title="Ulysses S. Grant">Ulysses S. Grant</a>'s candidacy for president, and Arthur raised funds for Grant's election <a href="/wiki/1868_United_States_presidential_election" title="1868 United States presidential election">in 1868</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197548_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197548-73">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The opposing Democratic machine in New York City, known as <a href="/wiki/Tammany_Hall" title="Tammany Hall">Tammany Hall</a>, worked for Grant's opponent, former New York Governor <a href="/wiki/Horatio_Seymour" title="Horatio Seymour">Horatio Seymour</a>; while Grant was victorious in the national vote, Seymour narrowly carried the state of New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197548_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197548-73">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Arthur began to devote more of his time to politics and less to law, and in 1869 he became counsel to the New York City Tax Commission, appointed when Republicans controlled the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Legislature" title="New York State Legislature">state legislature</a>. He remained at the job until 1870 at a salary of $10,000 a year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197549–50Howe42_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197549–50Howe42-74">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> Arthur resigned after Democrats controlled by William M. Tweed of Tammany Hall won a legislative majority, which meant they could name their own appointee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe42_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe42-77">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In 1871, Grant offered to name Arthur as <a href="/wiki/Commissioner_of_Internal_Revenue" title="Commissioner of Internal Revenue">Commissioner of Internal Revenue</a>, replacing <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Pleasonton" title="Alfred Pleasonton">Alfred Pleasonton</a>; Arthur declined the appointment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_Tribune&#39;&#39;18712_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_Tribune&#39;&#39;18712-78">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1870, President Grant gave Conkling control over New York <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">patronage</a>, including the Custom House at the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_New_York_and_New_Jersey#History" title="Port of New York and New Jersey">Port of New York</a>. Having become friendly with Murphy over their shared love of horses during summer vacations on the <a href="/wiki/Jersey_Shore" title="Jersey Shore">Jersey Shore</a>, in July of that year, Grant appointed him to the Collector's position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197551–53Howe44–45_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197551–53Howe44–45-79">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Murphy's reputation as a <a href="/wiki/War_profiteer" class="mw-redirect" title="War profiteer">war profiteer</a> and his association with Tammany Hall made him unacceptable to many of his own party, but Conkling convinced the Senate to confirm him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197551–53Howe44–45_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197551–53Howe44–45-79">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The Collector was responsible for hiring hundreds of workers to collect the tariffs due at the United States' busiest port. Typically, these jobs were dispensed to adherents of the political machine responsible for appointing the Collector. Employees were required to make political contributions (known as "assessments") back to the machine, which made the job a highly coveted political plum.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182-80">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Murphy's unpopularity only increased as he replaced workers loyal to Senator <a href="/wiki/Reuben_Fenton" title="Reuben Fenton">Reuben Fenton</a>'s faction of the Republican party with those loyal to Conkling's.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197557–58_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197557–58-81">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, the pressure to replace Murphy grew too great, and Grant asked for his resignation in December 1871.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197557–58_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197557–58-81">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Grant offered the position to <a href="/wiki/John_Augustus_Griswold" title="John Augustus Griswold">John Augustus Griswold</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Orton_(businessman)" title="William Orton (businessman)">William Orton</a>, each of whom declined and recommended Arthur.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDoyle_&amp;_Swaney188_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoyle_&amp;_Swaney188-82">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Grant then nominated Arthur, with the <i>New York Times</i> commenting, "his name very seldom rises to the surface of metropolitan life and yet moving like a mighty undercurrent this man during the last 10 years has done more to mold the course of the Republican Party in this state than any other one man in the country."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197560Howe46–47_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197560Howe46–47-83">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Senate confirmed Arthur's appointment; as Collector he controlled nearly a thousand jobs and received compensation as great as any federal officeholder.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182-80">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Arthur's salary was initially $6,500, but senior customs employees were compensated additionally by the "moiety" system, which awarded them a percentage of the cargoes seized and fines levied on importers who attempted to evade the tariff.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197559,_63,_85–86_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197559,_63,_85–86-84">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In total, his income came to more than $50,000—more than the president's salary, and more than enough for him to enjoy fashionable clothes and a lavish lifestyle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197559,_63,_85–86_84-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197559,_63,_85–86-84">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup> Among those who dealt with the Custom House, Arthur was one of the era's more popular collectors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197568_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197568-86">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> He got along with his subordinates and, since Murphy had already filled the staff with Conkling's adherents, he had few occasions to fire anyone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197569–70_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197569–70-87">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> He was also popular within the Republican party as he efficiently collected campaign assessments from the staff and placed party leaders' friends in jobs as positions became available.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197571–73_72-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197571–73-72">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Arthur had a better reputation than Murphy, but reformers still criticized the patronage structure and the moiety system as corrupt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182_80-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197561–67Schwartz182-80">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> A rising tide of reform within the party caused Arthur to rename the financial extractions from employees as "voluntary contributions" in 1872, but the concept remained, and the party reaped the benefit of controlling government jobs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197576–77_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197576–77-88">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In that year, reform-minded Republicans formed the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Liberal Republican Party (United States)">Liberal Republican</a> party and voted against Grant, but he was <a href="/wiki/1872_United_States_presidential_election" title="1872 United States presidential election">re-elected</a> in spite of their opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197578–79_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197578–79-89">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, the movement for civil service reform continued to chip away at Conkling's patronage machine; in 1874 Custom House employees were found to have improperly assessed fines against an importing company as a way to increase their own incomes, and Congress reacted, repealing the moiety system and putting the staff, including Arthur, on regular salaries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197579–84Howe49_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197579–84Howe49-90">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> As a result, his income dropped to $12,000 a year—more than his nominal boss, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a>, but far less than what he had previously received.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197579–84Howe49_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197579–84Howe49-90">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Clash_with_Hayes">Clash with Hayes</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Cartoon of a man kicking another man into the street" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/250px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="202" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/375px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg/500px-Arthur_NYcustoms_house_cartoon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1567" data-file-height="1269" /></a><figcaption>A cartoon depicting President <a href="/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes" title="Rutherford B. Hayes">Rutherford B. Hayes</a> kicking Arthur out of the New York Custom House</figcaption></figure>
<p>Arthur's four-year term as Collector expired on December 10, 1875, and Conkling, then among the most powerful politicians in Washington, arranged his protégé's reappointment by President Grant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197587–89_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197587–89-91">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In 1876, Conkling was a candidate for president at the <a href="/wiki/1876_Republican_National_Convention" title="1876 Republican National Convention">1876 Republican National Convention</a>, but the nomination was won by reformer <a href="/wiki/Rutherford_B._Hayes" title="Rutherford B. Hayes">Rutherford B. Hayes</a> on the seventh ballot.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves197595–96Karabell26–27_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves197595–96Karabell26–27-92">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Arthur and the machine gathered campaign funds with their usual zeal, but Conkling limited his own campaign activities for Hayes to a few speeches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975100–105_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975100–105-93">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Hayes's opponent, New York Governor <a href="/wiki/Samuel_J._Tilden" title="Samuel J. Tilden">Samuel J. Tilden</a>, carried New York and won the popular vote nationwide, but after the resolution of several months of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_Commission_(United_States)" title="Electoral Commission (United States)">disputes over twenty electoral votes</a> (from Florida, Louisiana, Oregon, and South Carolina), Hayes was declared the winner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975106–107_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975106–107-94">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes entered office with a pledge to reform the patronage system; in 1877, he and Treasury Secretary <a href="/wiki/John_Sherman" title="John Sherman">John Sherman</a> made Conkling's machine the primary target.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975100–105_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975100–105-93">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Sherman ordered a commission led by John Jay to investigate the New York Custom House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom318–319_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom318–319-95">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Jay, with whom Arthur had collaborated in the <i>Lemmon</i> case two decades earlier, suggested that the Custom House was overstaffed with political appointments, and that 20% of the employees were expendable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom322–325Reeves1975118–119Howe68–69_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom322–325Reeves1975118–119Howe68–69-96">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Sherman was less enthusiastic about the reforms than Hayes and Jay, but he approved the commission's report and ordered Arthur to make the personnel reductions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975119–120_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975119–120-97">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Arthur appointed a committee of Custom House workers to determine where the cuts were to be made and, after a written protest, carried them out.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–122_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–122-98">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Notwithstanding his cooperation, the Jay Commission issued a second report critical of Arthur and other Custom House employees, and subsequent reports urging a complete reorganization.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–122_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–122-98">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hayes further struck at the heart of the <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils system</a> by issuing an <a href="/wiki/Executive_order" title="Executive order">executive order</a> that forbade assessments, and barred federal office holders from "...tak[ing] part in the management of political organizations, caucuses, conventions, or election campaigns."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom322–325Reeves1975121_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom322–325Reeves1975121-99">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Arthur and his subordinates, Naval Officer <a href="/wiki/Alonzo_B._Cornell" title="Alonzo B. Cornell">Alonzo B. Cornell</a> and Surveyor <a href="/wiki/George_H._Sharpe" title="George H. Sharpe">George H. Sharpe</a>, refused to obey the president's order; Sherman encouraged Arthur to resign, offering him appointment by Hayes to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_France" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to France">consulship in Paris</a> in exchange, but Arthur refused.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–123_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975121–123-100">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In September 1877, Hayes demanded the three men's resignations, which they refused to give.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975123_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975123-101">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Hayes then submitted the appointment of <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt_Sr." title="Theodore Roosevelt Sr.">Theodore Roosevelt Sr.</a>, <a href="/wiki/L._Bradford_Prince" title="L. Bradford Prince">L. Bradford Prince</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Atkins_Merritt" title="Edwin Atkins Merritt">Edwin Merritt</a> (all supporters of Conkling's rival <a href="/wiki/William_M._Evarts" title="William M. Evarts">William M. Evarts</a>) to the Senate for confirmation as their replacements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom352Reeves1975125–126_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom352Reeves1975125–126-102">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The Senate's Commerce Committee, chaired by Conkling, unanimously rejected all the nominees; the full Senate rejected Roosevelt by a vote of 31–25<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> and similarly turned down the nomination of Prince by the same margin, later confirming Merritt only because Sharpe's term had expired.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom353–355Reeves1975126–131_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom353–355Reeves1975126–131-104">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's job was spared only until July 1878, when Hayes took advantage of a Congressional recess to fire him and Cornell, replacing them with the <a href="/wiki/Recess_appointment" title="Recess appointment">recess appointment</a> of Merritt and <a href="/wiki/Silas_W._Burt" title="Silas W. Burt">Silas W. Burt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom370–371Reeves1975136–137_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom370–371Reeves1975136–137-105">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;i&#93;</a></sup> Hayes again offered Arthur the position of <a href="/wiki/Consul_(representative)" title="Consul (representative)">consul general</a> in Paris as a <a href="/wiki/Face_(sociological_concept)" title="Face (sociological concept)">face-saving</a> consolation; Arthur again declined, as Hayes knew he probably would.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom354_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom354-108">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Conkling opposed the confirmation of Merritt and Burt when the Senate reconvened in February 1879, but Merritt was approved by a vote of 31–25, as was Burt by 31–19, giving Hayes his most significant civil service reform victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom382–384Reeves1975138–148_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom382–384Reeves1975138–148-109">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Arthur immediately took advantage of the resulting free time to work for the election of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Cooper_(mayor)" title="Edward Cooper (mayor)">Edward Cooper</a> as New York City's next mayor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe85_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe85-110">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> In September 1879 Arthur became chairman of the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Republican_State_Committee" title="New York Republican State Committee">New York State Republican Executive Committee</a>, a post in which he served until October 1881.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Sun&quot;_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Sun&quot;-111">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Boston_Post&quot;_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Boston_Post&quot;-112">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> In the state elections of 1879, he and Conkling worked to ensure that the Republican nominees for state offices would be men of Conkling's faction, who had become known as Stalwarts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Peskin704_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Peskin704-113">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> They were successful, but narrowly, as Cornell was nominated for governor by a vote of 234–216.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Peskin704_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Peskin704-113">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Arthur and Conkling campaigned vigorously for the Stalwart ticket and, owing partly to a splintering of the Democratic vote, were victorious.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Howe96–99_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975153–155Howe96–99-114">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Arthur and the machine had rebuked Hayes and their intra-party rivals, but Arthur had only a few days to enjoy his triumph when, on January 12, 1880, his wife died suddenly while he was in Albany organizing the political agenda for the coming year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975158–159Karabell38–39_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975158–159Karabell38–39-115">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Arthur felt devastated, and perhaps guilty, and never remarried.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe98–99Karabell38–39_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe98–99Karabell38–39-116">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1880">Election of 1880</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election" title="1880 United States presidential election">1880 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1880RepublicanPoster.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/1880RepublicanPoster.png/250px-1880RepublicanPoster.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/1880RepublicanPoster.png/375px-1880RepublicanPoster.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/1880RepublicanPoster.png/500px-1880RepublicanPoster.png 2x" data-file-width="984" data-file-height="680" /></a><figcaption>Garfield–Arthur campaign poster</figcaption></figure>
<p>Conkling and his fellow Stalwarts, including Arthur, wished to follow up their 1879 success at the <a href="/wiki/1880_Republican_National_Convention" title="1880 Republican National Convention">1880 Republican National Convention</a> by securing the presidential nomination for their ally, ex-President Grant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975160–165_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975160–165-117">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Their opponents in the Republican party, known as <a href="/wiki/Half-Breed_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Half-Breed (politics)">Half-Breeds</a>, concentrated their efforts on <a href="/wiki/James_G._Blaine" title="James G. Blaine">James G. Blaine</a>, a senator from Maine who was more amenable to civil service reform.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975160–165_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975160–165-117">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Neither candidate commanded a majority of delegates and, deadlocked after thirty-six ballots, the convention turned to a <a href="/wiki/Dark_horse" title="Dark horse">dark horse</a>, James A. Garfield, an Ohio Congressman and Civil War general who was neither Stalwart nor Half-Breed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975177–178Howe107–108Karabell39–40_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975177–178Howe107–108Karabell39–40-118">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Garfield and his supporters knew they would face a difficult election without the support of the New York Stalwarts and decided to offer one of them the vice presidential nomination.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell41Reeves1975178_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell41Reeves1975178-119">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Levi_P._Morton" title="Levi P. Morton">Levi P. Morton</a>, the first choice of Garfield's supporters, consulted with Conkling, who advised him to decline, which he did.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe107–108_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe107–108-120">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> They next approached Arthur, and Conkling advised him to also reject the nomination, believing the Republicans would lose.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179–181_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179–181-121">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Arthur thought otherwise and accepted. According to a purported eyewitness account by journalist William C. Hudson, Conkling and Arthur argued, with Arthur telling Conkling, "The office of the Vice-President is a greater honor than I ever dreamed of attaining."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179–181_121-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179–181-121">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;j&#93;</a></sup> Conkling eventually relented, and campaigned for the <a href="/wiki/Ticket_(election)" title="Ticket (election)">ticket</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975190–194_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975190–194-125">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As expected, the election was close. The Democratic nominee, General <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott_Hancock" title="Winfield Scott Hancock">Winfield Scott Hancock</a> was popular, and, having avoided taking definitive positions on most issues of the day, he had not offended any pivotal constituencies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJordan292–305_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJordan292–305-126">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> As Republicans had done since the end of the Civil War, Garfield and Arthur initially focused their campaign on the "<a href="/wiki/Bloody_shirt" class="mw-redirect" title="Bloody shirt">bloody shirt</a>"—the idea that returning Democrats to office would undo the victory of the Civil War and reward <a href="/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States" title="Secession in the United States">secessionists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975194–196Jordan294–295_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975194–196Jordan294–295-127">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1880.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/ElectoralCollege1880.svg/280px-ElectoralCollege1880.svg.png" decoding="async" width="280" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/ElectoralCollege1880.svg/420px-ElectoralCollege1880.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/ElectoralCollege1880.svg/560px-ElectoralCollege1880.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1880 electoral vote results</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the war fifteen years in the past and Union generals at the head of both tickets, the tactic was less effective than the Republicans hoped.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975194–196Jordan294–295_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975194–196Jordan294–295-127">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Realizing this, they adjusted their approach to claim that Democrats would lower the country's <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">protective tariff</a>, which would allow cheaper manufactured goods to be imported from Europe, and thereby put thousands out of work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196–197Jordan297–302_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196–197Jordan297–302-128">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> This argument struck home in the swing states of New York and Indiana, where many were employed in manufacturing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196–197Jordan297–302_128-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196–197Jordan297–302-128">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Hancock did not help his own cause when, in an attempt to remain neutral on the tariff, he said that "[t]he tariff question is a local question", which only made him appear uninformed about an important issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196Jordan301_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975196Jordan301-129">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Candidates for high office did not personally campaign in those days, but as state Republican chairman, Arthur played a part in the campaign in his usual fashion: overseeing the effort in New York and raising money.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975198–202_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975198–202-130">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The funds were crucial in the close election, and winning his home state of New York was critical.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204-131">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The Republicans carried New York by 20,000 votes and, in an election with the largest turnout of qualified voters ever recorded—78.4%—they won the nationwide popular vote by just 7,018 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204_131-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204-131">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">Electoral College</a> result was more decisive—214 to 155—and Garfield and Arthur were elected.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204_131-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975203–204-131">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Vice_presidency_.281881.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Vice_presidency_(1881)">Vice presidency (1881)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_of_Chester_A._Arthur" title="Inauguration of Chester A. Arthur">Inauguration of Chester A. Arthur</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CAArthur-oathofoffice_(edit).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg/220px-CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="247" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg/330px-CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg/440px-CAArthur-oathofoffice_%28edit%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="837" data-file-height="941" /></a><figcaption>Arthur taking the oath of office as administered by Judge <a href="/wiki/John_R._Brady" title="John R. Brady">John R. Brady</a> at <a href="/wiki/Chester_A._Arthur_Home" title="Chester A. Arthur Home">Arthur's home</a> in New York City, September 20, 1881</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the election, Arthur worked in vain to persuade Garfield to fill certain positions with his fellow New York Stalwarts—especially that of the Secretary of the Treasury; the Stalwart machine received a further rebuke when Garfield appointed Blaine, Conkling's arch-enemy, as Secretary of State.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975205–207_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975205–207-132">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> The running mates, never close, detached as Garfield continued to freeze out the Stalwarts from his patronage. Arthur's status in the administration diminished when, a month before inauguration day, he gave a speech before reporters suggesting the election in Indiana, a <a href="/wiki/Swing_state" title="Swing state">swing state</a>, had been won by Republicans through illegal machinations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975213–216Karabell52–53_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975213–216Karabell52–53-133">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Garfield ultimately appointed a Stalwart, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lemuel_James" title="Thomas Lemuel James">Thomas Lemuel James</a>, to be Postmaster General, but the cabinet fight and Arthur's ill-considered speech left the President and Vice President clearly estranged when they took office on March 4, 1881.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975216–219Karabell54–56_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975216–219Karabell54–56-134">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
