<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Chester_A._Arthur" title="List of federal judges appointed by Chester A. Arthur">Chester A. Arthur judicial appointments</a></div>
<p>Arthur made appointments to fill two vacancies on the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Supreme Court">United States Supreme Court</a>. The first vacancy arose in July 1881 with the death of <a href="/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Associate Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States">Associate Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Nathan_Clifford" title="Nathan Clifford">Nathan Clifford</a>, a Democrat who had been a member of the Court since before the Civil War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Arthur nominated <a href="/wiki/Horace_Gray" title="Horace Gray">Horace Gray</a>, a distinguished jurist from the <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts_Supreme_Judicial_Court" title="Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court">Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court</a> to replace him, and the nomination was easily confirmed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Gray would serve on the Court for over 20 years until resigning in 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-TLHall2001_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TLHall2001-226">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> The second vacancy occurred when Associate Justice <a href="/wiki/Ward_Hunt" title="Ward Hunt">Ward Hunt</a> retired in January 1882. Arthur first nominated his old political boss, <a href="/wiki/Roscoe_Conkling" title="Roscoe Conkling">Roscoe Conkling</a>; he doubted that Conkling would accept, but felt obligated to offer a high office to his former patron.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> The Senate confirmed the nomination but, as expected, Conkling declined it,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> the last time a confirmed nominee declined an appointment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Supreme_Court_Nominations&quot;_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Supreme_Court_Nominations&quot;-227">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Senator George Edmunds was Arthur's next choice, but he declined to be considered.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDoenecke76_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoenecke76-228">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Instead, Arthur nominated <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Blatchford" title="Samuel Blatchford">Samuel Blatchford</a>, who had been a judge on the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Second_Circuit" title="United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit">Second Circuit Court of Appeals</a> for the prior four years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Blatchford accepted, and his nomination was approved by the Senate within two weeks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195_225-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975260–261Howe195-225">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Blatchford served on the Court until his death in 1893.
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281885.E2.80.931886.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1885–1886)">Post-presidency (1885–1886)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Bronze statue of a man in a city park" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg/170px-Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg/255px-Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg/340px-Chester_A._Arthur_statue_by_Bissell_jeh.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2304" data-file-height="3888" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Chester_Alan_Arthur_(sculpture)" class="mw-redirect" title="Chester Alan Arthur (sculpture)"><i>Chester A. Arthur</i> statue</a> at <a href="/wiki/Madison_Square" class="mw-redirect" title="Madison Square">Madison Square</a> in New York City, Bissell <i>1898</i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Arthur left office in 1885 and returned to his New York City home. Two months before the end of his term, several New York Stalwarts approached him to request that he run for United States Senate, but he declined, preferring to return to his old law practice at Arthur, Knevals &amp; Ransom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975412–414_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975412–414-229">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> His health limited his activity with the firm, and Arthur served only <a href="/wiki/Of_counsel" title="Of counsel">of counsel</a>. He took on few assignments with the firm and was often too ill to leave his house.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418-230">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> He managed a few public appearances until the end of 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418_230-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418-230">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h3>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg/220px-Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg/330px-Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg/440px-Chester_Arthur_grave.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Arthur's grave at <a href="/wiki/Albany_Rural_Cemetery" title="Albany Rural Cemetery">Albany Rural Cemetery</a> in Menands, New York</figcaption></figure>
<p>After spending the summer of 1886 in <a href="/wiki/New_London,_Connecticut" title="New London, Connecticut">New London, Connecticut</a>, he returned home where he became seriously ill, and on November 16, ordered nearly all of his papers, both personal and official, burned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418_230-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418-230">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;q&#93;</a></sup> The next morning, Arthur suffered a <a href="/wiki/Cerebral_hemorrhage" class="mw-redirect" title="Cerebral hemorrhage">cerebral hemorrhage</a> and never regained consciousness. He died the following day, on November 18, at the age of 57.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418_230-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418-230">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> On November 22, a private funeral was held at the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Heavenly_Rest" title="Church of the Heavenly Rest">Church of the Heavenly Rest</a> in New York City, attended by President Cleveland and ex-President Hayes, among other notables.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975418–419_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975418–419-233">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> Arthur was buried with his family members and ancestors in the <a href="/wiki/Albany_Rural_Cemetery" title="Albany Rural Cemetery">Albany Rural Cemetery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Menands,_New_York" title="Menands, New York">Menands, New York</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;r&#93;</a></sup> He was laid beside his wife in a <a href="/wiki/Sarcophagus" title="Sarcophagus">sarcophagus</a> on a large corner of the plot.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418_230-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975416–418-230">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In 1889, a monument was placed on Arthur's burial plot by sculptor <a href="/wiki/Ephraim_Keyser" title="Ephraim Keyser">Ephraim Keyser</a> of New York, consisting of a giant bronze female angel figure placing a bronze palm leaf on a granite sarcophagus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_New_York_Times&#39;&#39;1894_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_New_York_Times&#39;&#39;1894-236">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's post-presidency was the second-shortest of all presidents who lived past their presidencies, after that of <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> who died just three months after leaving office.<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<p>Several <a href="/wiki/Grand_Army_of_the_Republic" title="Grand Army of the Republic">Grand Army of the Republic</a> posts were named for Arthur, including <a href="/wiki/Goff,_Kansas" title="Goff, Kansas">Goff, Kansas</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Meeting_of_C._A._Arthur_Post,_No._411&quot;1_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Meeting_of_C._A._Arthur_Post,_No._411&quot;1-238">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Lawrence,_Nebraska" title="Lawrence, Nebraska">Lawrence, Nebraska</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Leading_Officials&quot;13_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Leading_Officials&quot;13-239">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Medford,_Oregon" title="Medford, Oregon">Medford, Oregon</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;In_the_Encampment_at_the_Reunion_at_Ashland&quot;3_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;In_the_Encampment_at_the_Reunion_at_Ashland&quot;3-240">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Ogdensburg,_Wisconsin" title="Ogdensburg, Wisconsin">Ogdensburg, Wisconsin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;GAR_Posts_by_State:_Wisconsin&quot;12_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;GAR_Posts_by_State:_Wisconsin&quot;12-241">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> On April 5, 1882, Arthur was elected to the District of Columbia Commandery of the <a href="/wiki/Military_Order_of_the_Loyal_Legion_of_the_United_States" title="Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States">Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States</a> (MOLLUS) as a Third Class Companion (insignia number 02430<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;Original_Civil_War_Officer_Members_of_MOLLUS&quot;_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;Original_Civil_War_Officer_Members_of_MOLLUS&quot;-242">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>), the honorary membership category for militia officers and civilians who made significant contributions to the war effort.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Loyal_Legion&quot;1_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&quot;The_Loyal_Legion&quot;1-243">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Union College awarded Arthur the <a href="/wiki/Honorary_degree" title="Honorary degree">honorary degree</a> of <a href="/wiki/Legum_Doctor" title="Legum Doctor">LL.D.</a> in 1883.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_the_State_of_New_York21–22_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_the_State_of_New_York21–22-244">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1898, the <a href="/wiki/Chester_Alan_Arthur_(sculpture)" class="mw-redirect" title="Chester Alan Arthur (sculpture)">Arthur memorial statue</a>—a fifteen-foot (4.6&#160;m), bronze figure of Arthur standing on a <a href="/wiki/Barre_Granite" class="mw-redirect" title="Barre Granite">Barre Granite</a> pedestal—was created by sculptor <a href="/wiki/George_Edwin_Bissell" title="George Edwin Bissell">George Edwin Bissell</a> and installed at <a href="/wiki/Madison_Square" class="mw-redirect" title="Madison Square">Madison Square</a>, in New York City.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419-245">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> The statue was dedicated in 1899 and unveiled by Arthur's sister, Mary Arthur McElroy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419_245-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419-245">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> At the dedication, Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/Elihu_Root" title="Elihu Root">Elihu Root</a> described Arthur as, "...wise in statesmanship and firm and effective in administration," while acknowledging that Arthur was isolated in office and unloved by his own party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419_245-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975419-245">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's unpopularity in life carried over into his assessment by historians and his reputation after leaving office disappeared.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell139_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell139-246">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> By 1935, historian George F. Howe said that Arthur had achieved "an obscurity in strange contrast to his significant part in American history."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe288_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe288-247">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> By 1975, however, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_C._Reeves" title="Thomas C. Reeves">Thomas C. Reeves</a> would write that Arthur's "appointments, if unspectacular, were unusually sound; the corruption and scandal that dominated business and politics of the period did not tarnish his administration."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975420_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975420-248">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> As 2004 biographer <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Karabell" title="Zachary Karabell">Zachary Karabell</a> wrote, although Arthur was "physically stretched and emotionally strained, he strove to do what was right for the country."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarabell139_246-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell139-246">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, Howe had earlier surmised, "Arthur adopted [a code] for his own political behavior but subject to three restraints: he remained to everyone a man of his word; he kept scrupulously free from corrupt graft; he maintained a personal dignity, affable and genial though he might be. These restraints&#160;... distinguished him sharply from the stereotype politician."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe290_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe290-249">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arthur's townhouse, the <a href="/wiki/Chester_A._Arthur_Home" title="Chester A. Arthur Home">Chester A. Arthur Home</a>, was sold to <a href="/wiki/William_Randolph_Hearst" title="William Randolph Hearst">William Randolph Hearst</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> Since 1944 it has been the location of <a href="/wiki/Kalustyan%27s" title="Kalustyan&#39;s">Kalustyan's</a> Spice Emporium.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Arthur was Vice President under <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a> and became President upon <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_James_A._Garfield" title="Assassination of James A. Garfield">Garfield's death</a> on September 19, 1881. This was prior to the adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twenty-fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twenty-fifth Amendment</a> in 1967, and a vacancy in the office of vice president was not filled until the next ensuing election and inauguration.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-4">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Some older sources list the date as October 5, 1830,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe5_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe5-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> but biographer <a href="/wiki/Thomas_C._Reeves" title="Thomas C. Reeves">Thomas C. Reeves</a> confirms that this is incorrect: Arthur claimed to be a year younger "out of simple vanity."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-22"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-22">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Arthur pronounced his middle name with the accent on the second syllable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves19755_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves19755-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-35"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-35">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Even if he <i>had</i> been born in Canada, Arthur might have still claimed to be a "natural born citizen" based on his mother having been born in and recently resided in the United States.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-37"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-37">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The <a href="/wiki/Twelfth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twelfth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twelfth Amendment to the United States Constitution</a> applies that clause, which specifically restricts presidential eligibility, to would-be vice presidents: "No person constitutionally ineligible to the office of President shall be eligible to that of Vice-President."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Among the facts that argue against Hinman's theories are the entries for Chester A. Arthur in several U.S. Censuses from before he was politically prominent, which list his birthplace as Vermont, and the entry of his birth in the Arthur family Bible, which also indicates Vermont as his birthplace. In addition, contemporary newspaper articles, including the 1871 stories about his appointment as Collector of the Port of New York, all indicate that he was born in Vermont, though some incorrectly give his birthplace as <a href="/wiki/Burlington,_Vermont" title="Burlington, Vermont">Burlington</a>. Hinman failed to explain why Arthur would have fabricated these records and the biographical information he provided to newspapers to conceal a Canadian birth when the only thing being born in Canada might possibly affect was Arthur's eligibility for the presidency, which no one at the time of his birth or in the years between his birth and his nomination for vice president in 1880 had any reason to think he would aspire to.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-76"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-76">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">$10,000 in 1870 is equal to $231,421 in present terms.<sup id="cite_ref-inflation-US_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inflation-US-75">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-85"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-85">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">$50,000 in 1871 is equal to $1.22&#160;million in present terms.<sup id="cite_ref-inflation-US_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inflation-US-75">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-107"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-107">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Charles_K._Graham" title="Charles K. Graham">Charles K. Graham</a> filled Merritt's former position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom370_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoogenboom370-106">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-124"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-124">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Biographer George Howe takes this exchange at face value,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe109_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe109-122">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> but later biographers suspect it may be apocryphal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179Karabell40–41_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975179Karabell40–41-123">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-139"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-139">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Before the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Seventeenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution</a>, Senators were elected by state legislatures.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-140"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-140">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Conkling and Pratt were ultimately denied re-election, being succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Elbridge_G._Lapham" title="Elbridge G. Lapham">Elbridge G. Lapham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Warner_Miller" title="Warner Miller">Warner Miller</a>, respectively.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-150"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-150">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">One presidential oath was administered by a state court judge, also in New York City by a New York State judge: <a href="/wiki/Robert_R._Livingston_(chancellor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert R. Livingston (chancellor)">Robert Livingston</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Chancellor of New York">Chancellor of New York</a>, <a href="/wiki/First_inauguration_of_George_Washington" title="First inauguration of George Washington">administered the first presidential oath</a> to <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> at <a href="/wiki/Federal_Hall" title="Federal Hall">Federal Hall</a> in 1789 (there were yet no federal judges). The only other presidential oath administered by someone other than a Federal justice or judge, <a href="/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Calvin_Coolidge" title="First inauguration of Calvin Coolidge">the first swearing in</a> of <a href="/wiki/Calvin_Coolidge" title="Calvin Coolidge">Calvin Coolidge</a> in 1923 (by his father <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin_Coolidge,_Sr." class="mw-redirect" title="John Calvin Coolidge, Sr.">John Calvin Coolidge, Sr.</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Justice_of_the_peace" title="Justice of the peace">justice of the peace</a> and <a href="/wiki/Notary_public" title="Notary public">notary public</a>, in the family home), was also re-taken in Washington due to questions about the validity of the first oath. This second oath taking was done in secret, and did not become public knowledge until <a href="/wiki/Harry_M._Daugherty" title="Harry M. Daugherty">Harry M. Daugherty</a> revealed it in 1932.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-155"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-155">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Arthur first offered the post to Edwin D. Morgan, who had been his patron in New York; Morgan was confirmed by the Senate, but declined on the grounds of age. He died in 1883.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975254_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975254-154">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-186"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-186">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The portion of the law denying citizenship to Chinese-American children born in the United States was later found unconstitutional in <i><a href="/wiki/United_States_v._Wong_Kim_Ark" title="United States v. Wong Kim Ark">United States v. Wong Kim Ark</a></i> in 1898.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-194"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-194">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Earlier in 1874, during the Grant administration, Congress approved funds to rebuild four <a href="/wiki/Monitor_(warship)" title="Monitor (warship)">monitors</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/USS_Puritan_(BM-1)" title="USS Puritan (BM-1)">Puritan</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/USS_Amphitrite_(BM-2)" title="USS Amphitrite (BM-2)">Amphitrite</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/USS_Monadnock_(BM-3)" title="USS Monadnock (BM-3)">Monadnock</a></i>, and <i><a href="/wiki/USS_Terror_(BM-4)" title="USS Terror (BM-4)">Terror</a></i>), which had lain uncompleted since 1877.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1975342–343Abbot346–347_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1975342–343Abbot346–347-193">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-232"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-232">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">A small number of Arthur's papers survived and passed to his grandson, <a href="/wiki/Gavin_Arthur" title="Gavin Arthur">Gavin Arthur</a> (born Chester Alan Arthur III), who allowed Arthur's biographer, Thomas C. Reeves, to examine them in the 1970s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReeves1972&#39;&#39;passim&#39;&#39;_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1972&#39;&#39;passim&#39;&#39;-231">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-235"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-235">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">On his grave marker, his birth year is incorrectly given as 1830.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
